# Observation first, then translation: a boulder at a tarn, at dusk

Everything here lives in `~/work/pixelart-studies/pedagogy/observation/`.

| what | where |
|---|---|
| reference photos (28, with credits) | `refs/r01..r28*.jpg`, `refs/credits.tsv` (candidates in `refs/cand*/`) |
| value block-ins of the photos | `studies/blockin_*.png` (made by `blockin.py`) |
| measured light profiles | `studies/measurements.png` (made by `studies_profiles.py`) |
| Ferrari at native size, and zoomed crops | `ferrari/native.png`, `ferrari/*.png`; contour stats in `ferrari_contours.py` |
| translation studies: photo, Ferrari, mine, at 8x | `studies/translate_{A..E}.png` (made by `translate_studies.py`, primitives in `pix.py`) |
| the method | `tarn.py` (the first, abandoned attempt is kept as `tarn_v1_first_attempt.py`) |
| parameter sheets | `out/sheet_size.png`, `sheet_light.png`, `sheet_distance.png`, `sheet_seed.png` (made by `sheets.py`) |
| pixel statistics, and my cell next to Ferrari | `compare_stats.py`, `out/compare_ferrari_vs_mine.png` |

Commands: `nice -n 10 python3 tarn.py --size large --light back --distance close --seed 3`
and `nice -n 10 python3 sheets.py` (4 worker processes, one thread each).

---

## 1. The photographs

Wikimedia Commons is poor for "boulder at a tarn at dusk" as one photo. I split the subject into
its parts and gathered each one separately. These are the ones I actually used:

- **Light wrapping granite, sun low from the left:** r01–r05 (Joshua Tree, public domain);
  r07–r09 (Monsanto granite at dusk under a violet sky).
- **A boulder's foot in grass, the contact:** r10 (boulder by Lochan na h-Earba, with an undercut
  at its foot), r13 (an erratic sitting in a lawn).
- **Boulders at still water, and their reflections:** r16, r18 (a stone in flat water), r19
  (Blea Tarn: a boulder at the lip of a grass bank, reflected, with sedge coming out of the water).
- **Grass banks and tussocks against water, and grass in low sun:** r20, r22, r23.
- **Dusk water carrying the sky:** r21, r24, r25 (Lake Cerknica, pink over violet).
- **Distance:** r11 and r12 (the same kind of boulder at 30–60 m on a moor).

## 2. What is actually there

Numbers are CIE L* sampled from the photos (`studies/measurements.png`, and point samples logged
in the session).

### Light on a rounded boulder (r02, r19, r10)

- **The lit side is a plateau, not a gradient.** Along a scanline in r02 the lit face holds at
  L*≈60–80 across most of its width. Rough granite scatters light broadly, so its lit side looks
  flatter than a Lambert sphere.
- **The terminator is a cliff.** L* drops from about 60 to 25 within a few percent of the
  boulder's width, and just past the drop there is a **core band** that is darker than the rest
  of the shadow side (terminator L*≈25, shadow side further round L*≈37 in r02).
- **The shadow side is lit by bounce and is warm.** Its far part picks up light again from the
  lit ground (r02: from L*≈25 back up to 37, and more orange). At dusk the sky is also a light:
  upward-facing planes in shadow turn violet and lighter.
- **Contact.** The darkest value in the whole boulder sits at the line where it meets the ground
  (r02 crevice L*≈33 against a lit face at 58; r19 contact L*≈31 against a lit top at 60). Where
  the rock overhangs (r10), the pocket underneath is the darkest thing in the picture.
- **The cast shadow** of a 4–5° sun is very long. Low grass inside it goes dark olive and loses
  its warm highlights, which makes it a different colour family, not just a darker version.

### Grass masses (r20, r22, r23)

- Grass is a **volume of near-vertical blades**. With low sun, what decides lit versus shadow
  grass is cast shadow, not the tilt of the ground: a whole tussock can be lit on a slope that
  faces away from the sun.
- Light collects on **crests**: the top edge of every mass and its sun-facing shoulder. The base
  and the body sink into shadow (r20 block-in: tussock tops at L*≈50–65, bodies at 18–30).
- **Texture has a direction and changes with depth.** I measured the ratio of horizontal to
  vertical gradient: in r22 it rises from 1.1 (isotropic) in the distance to 1.45 in the
  foreground. Near grass reads as vertical strokes. Far grass reads as stipple.
- **Facing the sun**, you see the shadowed side of each blade, with glowing tips from light
  shining through them.

### Where the bank meets the water, and reflections (r19, r25, r18)

- **Reflections are darker than what they reflect.** Sky mid band L*=86 against its reflection
  at 55; hill 19 against 12.5; boulder shadow side 28 against 15 (r19); stone 11 against 5 (r18).
  Water is a Fresnel mirror, and near the viewer it shows the dark water body.
- **Reflections show the underside.** You look down at the bank and the mirror looks up at it,
  so the reflection is shorter and made mostly of the shadowed faces.
- **At the far waterline there is a light line**, the water right at the edge reflecting sky at
  a grazing angle (a spike in the r25 profile). **Right under a near grass lip it is dark**
  (overhang shadow).
- **Still water is broken into horizontal streaks** by faint ripples and wind-lines, which
  reflect a slightly higher, lighter part of the sky.

### Distance (r11, r12; resampling in `measurements.png` panel D)

- A 2 m boulder at 40 m is about 6 px tall at this camera. At 10 px it still has light side,
  shadow side and contact. At 4 px it is two values and a dark base.
- Aerial perspective at dusk pushes far things toward the violet-pink of the horizon sky. The far
  treeline stays nearly black because it is backlit or in shadow.

### Value structure (`studies/blockin_*.png`)
The 2-value notan of every dusk photo is the same: **the sky and the water that mirrors it form
the light family; all land forms the dark family.** Inside the land, only sun-struck planes rise
into the middle values. This became the palette's first constraint.

## 3. How Ferrari translates these things

Measured on `ferrari/native.png`: the supplied PNG is his image at 2× pixel scale, so native
resolution is 634×469 with 172 colours.

- **Grass: one 3-colour family per lighting zone.** In the near-left lit grass, four colours make
  up 99% of the pixels: ochre base (48%), tan blades (32%), dark-green gaps (16%). In the
  shadowed near-right grass it is black (49%), dark olive (25%), darker olive (25%). The shadow
  zone is **a different family**, which matches the photos: cast-shadowed grass is not lit grass
  made darker.
- **Blades are 1-px lines** made of vertical runs averaging about 3 px, with horizontal runs of
  1.5 px (`crop_grass_tips_on_water.png`). Near the tips they lean in equal steps.
- **Crest bands.** Each lit mass has a 1–3 px band of its lightest tan directly under the top
  contour, and the lit colour is then stippled down into the shadow body with falling density
  (`treebase.png`, `island8.png`).
- **Contours resolve into clean runs** (`ferrari_contours.py`). Along the mountain ridge, 57% of
  run–step pairs are exactly 45° (1,1) or 2:1 (2,1), and consecutive runs repeat their length
  54% of the time. The left bank lip is 90% consistent. He holds a slope's step pattern until
  the form turns.
- **Rock is flat planes with a lit lip.** The sun-facing ridges of the mountain facets carry the
  lightest salmon as a 1–2 px edge. Planes are one colour, flecked with the neighbouring value.
- **Stones at the waterline** (`stones8.png`) are three or four flat values, the darkest as a line
  underneath. Their reflections are horizontal slabs one step darker.
- **Water and sky:** flat bands of adjacent ramp colours joined by 50% checkerboard strips.
  Reflections of things are horizontal runs, and light wind-lines cut across them.
- **Pixel statistics** (`compare_stats.py`): 4.7 colours per 8×8 tile, 20% isolated pixels,
  mean horizontal run 1.73 px, vertical run 2.05 px. The painting is dense with texture, but that
  texture sits inside a few large, clear shapes.

## 4. The translation studies (the step that made the method work)

My first program (`tarn_v1_first_attempt.py`) went straight from a physical render to palette
quantisation and blade scattering. It produced a smooth render that had been posterised, not a
painting (`out/t1.png`). After the critic's note, I went back and translated **one observed truth
at a time**, each shown beside its photo and beside Ferrari's nearest pixels at 8×:

- **D, light on a boulder** (`translate_D.png`). Painting each lighting zone as flat colour made
  cartoon cel-shading. The fix was a continuous ramp coordinate (1 contact, 2 core, 3 bounce
  shadow, 4 half/sky-lit top, 5 lit, 6 rim). The lit side and the shadow side get *separate
  ranges* with a jump between them, so dithering never crosses the terminator. Values dither only
  where they fall between colours, so planes stay flat, and the dither is clustered (2-px clumps),
  never salt-and-pepper. Added: a sun-side 1-px rim, a vertical weathering grain, a joint crack
  with a lit lip, and the darkest colour on the contact row.
- **A, the contact** (`translate_A.png`): the darkest line under the rock, a black undercut pocket
  on the shadow side, the cast shadow switching the grass to the shadow family, and tufts
  overlapping the rock's foot.
- **B, the lit top of a grass mass** (`translate_B.png`): contour through `clean_profile` (flat
  crest, 2:1 shoulders, 45° flanks), a crest band 3 px thick on the sun side thinning to 1 px,
  its lower edge notched, and the lit colour stippled downward. The first version drew a clean
  tan outline, which looked like a drawing, not grass.
- **C, the lip and its reflection** (`translate_C.png`): the reflection is mirrored and
  compressed, shifted one step darker, and resampled as 2–7 px horizontal runs. A light line
  marks the waterline at middle distance. A ragged edge fades the reflection into the sky-water.
  Wind-lines are solid 1-px strokes. Mirroring the bank's own dark lip line produced a doubled
  outline and had to be excluded.
- **E, near blades against water** (`translate_E.png`): each blade leans by runs that shorten
  toward the tip (5, 4, 3, 2, 1: a curve made only of clean steps). Gap strokes are short and
  vertical. Tan blades stand against the water.

The primitives these studies produced (`clean_profile`, `majority_clean`, clustered dither,
horizontal-run resampling, stroke blades) are what `tarn.py` uses.

## 5. The method (`tarn.py`)

**Stage 1, observe.** A small scene model at 320×180:

- a signed-distance granite corestone: a rounded box, tapered toward a dome, tilted, with broad
  weathering dents, optionally split by a joint, and sunk 22–36% into the bank;
- a bank heightfield with 3–4 m hummocks and 1 m tussocks;
- a near bank and a promontory lip, and the tarn out to a far shore 70–110 m away with conifers
  (mark-painted, heights in metres) and three layers of faceted peaks.

Light model: sun at 4.5°; sun colour warm orange; sky fill violet; bounce from lit grass,
arriving from below and away from the sun; soft shadows (grass visibility tested from the blade
tops); AO; blade translucency when back-lit. The water is a rippled Fresnel mirror that
ray-traces the scene again.

Output is a G-buffer: material, depth, normal, sun visibility, n·l, bounce, AO, height above
ground, what each water pixel reflects, and the wind-lines.

**Palette, 31 colours.** Every entry comes from the light model evaluated at a *canonical light
state*, so the palette is the observation, quantised deliberately:

- grass lit family: crest, tan, ochre, dark-green gap;
- grass shadow family: dark olive, darker olive, black; plus one mid tone for reflections;
- rock: contact, core, bounce shadow, sky-lit half, lit, rim;
- sky: 8 colours spaced evenly along the path from 35° down to the horizon;
- far: 4 colours, k-means of the mountains;
- water: 4 colours, the sky ramp darkened as Fresnel predicts;
- one accent (sun disk and glitter when back-lit, otherwise the hottest grass tip).

**Stage 2, translate.** Each material is painted with its study's vocabulary: sky bands with
checker strips; faceted mountains; mark-painted trees and far bank; water reflections as
horizontal runs one step darker, checker for mirrored sky, light and dark waterlines, solid
wind-lines; emergent sedge in the shallows (r19) with broken dark reflections; rock by ramp
coordinate with fall-line flecks, rim and contact. Grass is painted last: stippled family choice
from the light amount, crest bands on sun-facing tops, then 1-px blades far to near, depth-tested
so near tips overlap the water and the boulder's foot. Blade length is set by projecting 0.3 m
blades, which is how distance changes the grass from stroke to stipple, as observed.

**Parameters.** Size (1.1 / 1.9 / 2.9 m); light direction (sun azimuth −105°, 170°, +105°, −14°
relative to the view, so left and right are raking side-light); distance (5.5, 10, 40 m); seed
(boulder shape, dents, joint, sink depth, shoreline, bank position, tree density, peaks, ripples).

## 6. Honest comparison

**Against the photos, what carries over:**

- The dusk notan: sky and water light, land dark.
- The boulder's plateau-and-cliff light, with a core band, a warm bounce-lit shadow side, a
  sky-lit violet top, a sun-side rim and a black contact line (visible in the close row of
  `sheet_distance`).
- Reflections that are darker and show the underside. The back-lit row has the dark rock mass
  over a bright mirrored sky that r25 and r18 show.
- Lit versus shadow grass decided by cast shadows, and the view-dependent flip: facing the sun,
  the grass shows its shadowed side with glowing tips.
- Distance turning strokes into stipple, and the 40 m boulder shrinking to a few values, as in
  the resampling study.

**Against the photos, what is off:** the rock's surface is too clean at 10 m. Real granite at dusk
shows lichen mottling and a cooler, greyer hue than my salmon. The cast shadow of the boulder on
the grass is mostly hidden by the camera angle and rarely reads.

**Against Ferrari (`out/compare_ferrari_vs_mine.png`, same scale):**

- The micro-statistics now match closely: 4.3–4.8 colours per tile (his 4.7), 16–20% isolated
  pixels (his 20%), runs 1.9 × 2.1 px (his 1.7 × 2.0). Every cell uses 24–28 of 31 colours.
- **The biggest gap is structure, not texture.** His grass banks are big rounded masses, each
  split into a lit half and a shadow half with a crest band, and the whole picture is built from
  a few of those masses. My near grass is one even field, so the bottom third of every cell is
  texture with no shapes in it, and at 40 m this dominates the frame. The hummocks exist in my
  geometry, but at 4.5° sun and a 10° camera they barely change the lighting. Ferrari invents
  the masses. The next step is to paint the bank as designed masses: pick 2–4 mounds per cell,
  light them with the study-B recipe, and let the physical model decide only which side is lit.
- My water is a large, mostly flat violet area. His is full of the mirrored mountains and banks.
  My framing puts too much open water below the far shore and too little land around it.
- Contour cleaning is applied only to the far bank top and in the studies, not to the boulder
  silhouette or the grass masses in the full method.
- His palette is richer in the middle values, with more warm-to-violet steps in the land. Mine
  spends 8 entries on the sky, which is flatter than his.

**Were the sheets painted?** Yes at close range and at 10 m, especially the back-lit and raking
cells. The 40 m row is the weakest: it reads as a grass texture sample with a scene behind it.
